    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Yeah, thank you for, um, fitting me in. So... I guess the main thing that finally pushed me to come in—and I've been thinking about doing this for a few months now, honestly, but I just kept putting it off—is that I’m having a really hard time at my job. \n\nI work as an insurance claims clerk, processing policies, which means I'm basically at a computer all day. And the issue is, I just keep getting up from my workstation during the day. Like, all the time. Even when I know, I absolutely know, I’m supposed to stay put and just finish my claim reviews. It's so frustrating because I'll sit down with a batch of claims, and I know I have a quota to hit, you know, they track how many we process and how fast we do them... but I'll get maybe halfway through reading one, and I just end up walking away from my desk. \n\nI'll go to the breakroom to check the coffee pot, or I'll walk down the hall for no real reason, or I'll just stand up and wander over to the printer even if I didn't print anything. And I know I shouldn't be doing it, I know I need to just stay in my chair and get the reviews done, but it's like I can't just stay seated. I just keep leaving my desk, and then I have to come back and figure out where I even was on the claim, and it's really starting to mess with my ability to actually get through my work.\n\nDoctor: When you do manage to stay seated at your desk, are you able to focus and concentrate on reading through the insurance claims, or does your mind feel like it's wandering and jumping around even when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's... that's exactly it, actually. Even when I do manage to force myself to just stay in the chair, my brain is just absolutely all over the place. \n\nWe have this open-plan office setup, which honestly I think was a terrible idea for a claims department because the cubicle walls are so low you can hear literally everything. So if I'm sitting there, trying my absolute hardest to just stare at my screen and read through a bodily injury claim or whatever, and the people in the next pod over start talking about a completely different claim—like maybe they're trying to figure out a coverage limit or complaining about a specific client—my brain just completely latches onto their conversation. I can't tune it out. \n\nAnd it doesn't even have to be the office noise, honestly. Sometimes it's just my own head. I'll be right in the middle of a complex paragraph about property damage, and suddenly I'll remember that I need to pick up paper towels on the way home, or I'll wonder if I paid my electric bill, or I'll just start thinking about some random errand I have to run on the weekend. \n\nWhen that happens, it's like my attention just completely slides right off what I’m reading. My eyes are still moving over the words, you know? Like, I'm physically still looking at the document and scrolling. But nothing is actually going in. I'll get to the bottom of the page and realize I have absolutely no idea what I just looked at. So then I have to go back and reread the exact same section. And sometimes I have to reread it two, three, four times before the information actually sticks in my head, which just eats up so much time. It's so exhausting because it takes so much energy just to force my eyes to stay on the screen, and even then, if Brenda two desks down starts talking about her lunch, I'm suddenly fully invested in Brenda's lunch instead of the policy in front of me, and I have to start the whole rereading process all over again.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these difficulties with staying focused and sitting still? Was this something you struggled with back in school, or is this more of a recent problem that's developed over the past year or so?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because for a long time I really thought this was just a \"now\" problem, like maybe the open office or the boring claims were just finally getting to me. But when I really sit down and think about it... honestly, looking back, this definitely isn't brand new for me at all. Not even a little bit. \n\nI actually started thinking about this the other day, and I remembered how things were back when I was a kid. Like, way back in elementary school. I always felt like I was doing okay, you know, I wasn't failing or anything, but my parents kept all my old report cards in this big plastic bin in the basement, and I was looking through them a while ago. And it's crazy because every single year, it was the exact same story from every teacher. They would always write these little notes at the bottom, like, \"He's a very bright boy, very capable,\" but then the next sentence was always, always about how I was losing my worksheets. I mean, I would just shove papers into my desk or my backpack and they would just disappear into the void. \n\nAnd they also wrote about how I would constantly miss instructions. Like, the teacher would stand at the front of the room and explain exactly how to do the math assignment, and I would be looking right at her, but my brain was just... somewhere else entirely, probably thinking about recess or a cartoon or something. And then everyone would start working, and I'd just be sitting there completely lost, having to ask the kid next to me what we were supposed to be doing. \n\nThe teachers were always saying I needed constant reminders to actually finish my classwork, too. Because I'd start a worksheet, get maybe three problems in, and then I'd just start drawing in the margins or staring out the window, and the teacher would have to come tap on my desk and tell me to get back to work. So yeah, I guess when I really look at the whole picture, it's not just a recent thing at all. It's like I've always been this way, just kind of drifting off and losing track of things, even when I was just a little kid trying to get through third grade. It's just that now, instead of losing a spelling worksheet, I'm losing my place in a legal document, you know?\n\nDoctor: Besides the difficulty staying in your chair at work, have you noticed that you tend to be restless or fidgety in other situations too—like when you're watching TV at home, or sitting through a meeting, or even just trying to relax? Do your hands or legs tend to move around a lot?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah. Yeah, absolutely. It's... it's not just that I get up, it's that even when I am technically stuck in a chair, I'm never actually still. \n\nLike, we have these mandatory team meetings every Tuesday morning, and then sometimes they make us do these really long quarterly compliance trainings, right? They book out the big conference room on the second floor for those. And I go in there, and I always tell myself, \"Okay, just sit still, look professional, pretend you are locked in.\" Because I want to look focused, I really do. But man, within maybe five or ten minutes, my right leg is just going a mile a minute. I'll be bouncing my knee up and down under the table, completely unconsciously. Half the time I don't even realize I'm doing it until my knee bumps the underside of the table or I notice the water in somebody's glass vibrating a little bit. \n\nAnd then, you know, I'll catch myself and force my leg to stop. I'll press my foot flat on the floor and tell myself to just freeze. But the second I do that, the restlessness just moves somewhere else. I'll start shifting around in my chair endlessly. Those conference room chairs are those weird ergonomic mesh ones, and I just can't seem to find a position that feels right for more than two minutes. I'll cross my legs, then uncross them, lean to the left, sit back, lean forward on my elbows... I'm just constantly readjusting my weight. \n\nAnd the pens! If I have a pen in my hand—which I usually do, because we're supposed to be taking notes, even though they literally always just email us the PowerPoint deck afterwards anyway—I am just ruthlessly clicking it. Click, click, click, click. I actually had my supervisor, Dave, shoot me this really intense look across the table a couple of weeks ago because I was clicking my retractable pen over and over during a whole presentation on new underwriting guidelines. I felt terrible because I wasn't trying to be disruptive, my hands just needed something to do. \n\nSo yeah, even when I'm trying my absolute hardest to just look like a normal, attentive person in a meeting or a training, I'm bouncing, clicking, shifting... it's like the energy just has to leak out of me somehow. And it's the same at home, honestly. If I'm on the couch trying to watch a movie, I'm usually flipping my phone over and over in my hand or shaking my foot the entire time. I just can't seem to turn the motor off.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to your childhood—say, before you turned 12 years old—do you remember having these same kinds of problems with sitting still and paying attention, or did those issues really only start showing up once you were older?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, like I was just mentioning a minute ago when we were talking about those old report cards, it really wasn't a brand new thing for me at all. I mean, thinking specifically about before I was 12—so, you know, early elementary school—it was exactly that same stuff I was just talking about. \n\nLike I said, the teachers would always start off the report cards nice, writing that I was a \"bright boy\" or that I was \"very capable,\" but then it was just this laundry list of the exact same issues every single year. The losing worksheets thing was huge. I don't even know how I managed it, honestly, but my desk was just a complete black hole. I'd shove a paper in there and it would just get crushed under a million other things, just completely disappear. My mom used to get so frustrated because at parent-teacher conferences they'd always make me clean it out, and we'd find all these crumpled up, half-finished assignments from three weeks ago just stuffed in the back. \n\nAnd the missing instructions... yeah, that was definitely happening way back then, too. Like I was saying earlier, the teacher would be at the front of the room explaining a project or a math lesson, and I would be staring right at her, but my brain was just completely tuned out. I'd be watching a bug on the window or just daydreaming about whatever, and then suddenly the room gets loud and everyone is moving and opening their textbooks, and I'm just sitting there in a total panic because I have absolutely no idea what we're supposed to be doing or what page we're on. \n\nAnd then, of course, like I mentioned, they always had to give me constant reminders to actually finish my classwork. Because even if I did manage to figure out what to do, I'd do maybe one or two problems and then just start doodling in the margins or peeling the paper off my crayons. I had this one teacher in fourth grade, Mrs. Gable, who used to literally just walk by and tap on my desk with her knuckle every ten minutes. She wouldn't even say anything, just *tap tap*, because she knew if she didn't constantly pull my attention back to the paper, I would literally just stare at the wall until recess. So, yeah, it definitely goes way back before I was 12. It's pretty much always been there.\n\nDoctor: Looking back to those early school years—before you were 12—do you remember your parents or teachers ever expressing concern that these attention and restlessness issues were actually causing you problems with your grades, or getting into trouble, or keeping up with your schoolwork?\n\nPatient: Well, back then... I mean, they were definitely annoyed, and there were always those parent-teacher conferences where my mom would come home looking super stressed out because of the missing assignments and the messy desk. But the thing was, I wasn't really failing. I was smart enough that I could usually cram at the last second or guess my way through a test, so my grades were mostly okay—like, mostly Bs and Cs. So my parents and teachers were concerned about my potential, you know, always saying I wasn't \"living up to it\" or that I was being lazy, but it wasn't completely ruining my life back then. I wasn't getting suspended or held back or anything like that. I always just managed to scrape by.\n\nBut honestly, the reason I've been thinking about all this so much lately—and why I'm even sitting here talking to you today—is because of how much it's actually impacting my life *now*. Like, scraping by in fourth grade is one thing, but work... work is a totally different story, and it's completely falling apart on me. \n\nRemember how I mentioned my supervisor, Dave? The one who gave me the dirty look for clicking my pen? Well, it's gone way past just him being annoyed with me in meetings. He actually pulled me into his office last week for a formal one-on-one, and... well, he put me on an official performance coaching plan. Which is basically just HR corporate-speak for \"get it together right now or you're in major trouble.\" \n\nAnd it's all because my claim files are just completely falling behind. I have this huge queue of claims I'm supposed to be processing, and I just stare at the screen and can't make myself get through them fast enough, or I get distracted and lose my place and have to start reading the same file all over again. And worse than just being slow, Dave said my accuracy is being seriously questioned right now. I guess I've been missing key details in the policy documents, or putting the wrong codes into the system because my brain is just jumping all over the place and I'm rushing to catch up. \n\nAnd this is just... it's a total disaster for me, honestly. I've been at this company a while and I was really, really aiming for the senior adjuster track this year. It's a huge bump in pay, it's a better schedule, and I really wanted it. I thought I was next in line for it. But now, with this performance coaching thing on my record and my accuracy in the toilet, I am just terrified I'm going to lose that opportunity entirely. It just feels like my brain is literally costing me my career at this point, and I just... I can't seem to stop it from happening, no matter how hard I try to focus.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, did these attention and restlessness problems actually hold you back in any real way—like, did you fall behind your classmates academically, have trouble making friends, or get into trouble at school because of them? Or was it more just that your teachers were frustrated but you were still keeping up okay overall?\n\nPatient: Yeah, so, kind of like I was just saying a second ago, it was definitely more the second thing. I wasn't falling behind academically in any kind of permanent way. I mean, I never had to repeat a grade or go to summer school or anything drastic like that. \n\nLike I mentioned, I was getting mostly Bs and Cs because I could figure things out at the last possible second. I was basically the king of the panic-cram. Like, if we had a spelling test or a vocabulary quiz on a Friday, I wouldn't even look at the words Monday through Thursday. I'd lose the list, or forget it in my desk, and then Thursday night my mom would find out and be drilling me at the kitchen table while I was just completely overwhelmed and frustrated, but I'd still manage to pull off a B-minus or a C-plus the next morning. So on paper, I looked fine. I kept up okay, even if the process of getting there was a total mess behind the scenes. \n\nAnd as far as getting into trouble... I wasn't a bad kid at all. I wasn't getting sent to the principal's office for fighting or being defiant or anything. It was really just... exasperation. The teachers were just constantly exasperated with me. Like Mrs. Gable with her tapping on my desk. She wasn't furious with me, she was just tired of me staring at the window instead of my math sheet. And making friends was never really an issue either. I was pretty sociable—maybe a little too talkative when I was actually supposed to be reading quietly, which probably annoyed the teachers even more—but the other kids liked me fine. \n\nSo yeah, it didn't really hold me back back then. It was just a lot of friction, I guess you could call it. A lot of friction and annoyed adults, but no real, life-altering consequences. Not like now with Dave and the HR stuff. That's why it's all so jarring to me lately, because back then I could just skate by on being a \"bright boy\" who just needed to apply himself, but you can't really pull that off when you're 26 and messing up actual insurance claims, you know?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I'd lose the list, or forget it in my desk, and then Thursday night my mom would find out and be drilling me at the kitchen table while I was just completely overwhelmed and frustrated, but I'd still manage to pull off a B-minus or a C-plus the next morning. I wasn't getting sent to the principal's office for fighting or being defiant or anything. It was really just... exasperation. The teachers were just constantly exasperated with me. Like Mrs. Gable with her tapping on my desk. She wasn't furious with me, she was just tired of me staring at the window instead of my math sheet. And making friends was never really an issue either. I was pretty sociable—maybe a little too talkative when I was actually supposed to be reading quietly, which probably annoyed the teachers even more—but the other kids liked me fine. \n\nSo yeah, it didn't really hold me back back then. It was just a lot of friction, I guess you could call it. A lot of friction and annoyed adults, but no real, life-altering consequences. Not like now with Dave and the HR stuff. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Honestly, it really is just like I was saying a minute ago. I mean, I don't think they ever felt like it was ruining my life or anything like that. Because, you know, when it came to things I actually *wanted* to do? I was totally fine. Like, it never kept me from my activities or anything. If anything, my mom would get more annoyed because she’d see me out in the yard building this incredibly elaborate fort out of cardboard boxes for three hours straight, completely focused, or I'd be playing video games until dinner, and then she’d be like, "Why can't you just do that with your spelling worksheet?" So it definitely wasn't stopping me from having fun or doing the stuff I cared about. 

And at home... I mean, yeah, like I said, my mom was stressed about the messy desk and those Thursday night cram sessions, but it wasn't causing, like, massive family blowouts. I wasn't getting grounded for weeks on end or anything. It was just this constant, low-level annoyance for her. She'd sigh a lot, you know? Like, "Here we go again with the missing paper." But we still had a totally normal family life otherwise. We went on camping trips, we watched movies together. It wasn't this dark cloud hanging over the whole house. My dad worked a lot, so he wasn't as involved in the day-to-day homework battles, but even he just kind of saw it as me being a bit scatterbrained. 

And with other kids, like I mentioned, I was always pretty social. I had my group of friends, we rode bikes around the neighborhood, traded cards, all that normal kid stuff. None of them cared if I lost my math homework or if Mrs. Gable had to tap on my desk. Kids don't really pay attention to that stuff unless you're, like, throwing chairs or something, which I never did. 

So, no, I really don't remember anyone saying it was causing *real* damage to my life back then. It was just... I was just the disorganized kid who daydreamed too much. That was my label. It's just so crazy to think about how different it is now, because back then the worst thing that happened was my mom giving me a lecture at the kitchen table, and now I'm literally sitting in HR meetings sweating through my shirt because Dave is looking at my claim processing times and telling me I'm missing policy details. It's just... it's a completely different world now. When you're a kid, people just kind of cover for you or push you along, but nobody is doing that for me at the insurance firm.

I just keep leaving my desk, and then I have to come back and figure out where I even was on the claim, and it's really starting to mess with my ability to actually get through my work.\n\nDoctor: When you do manage to stay seated at your desk, are you able to focus and concentrate on reading through the insurance claims, or does your mind feel like it's wandering and jumping around even when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's... that's exactly it, actually. Even when I do manage to force myself to just stay in the chair, my brain is just absolutely all over the place. \n\nWe have this open-plan office setup, which honestly I think was a terrible idea for a claims department because the cubicle walls are so low you can hear literally everything. So if I'm sitting there, trying my absolute hardest to just stare at my screen and read through a bodily injury claim or whatever, and the people in the next pod over start talking about a completely different claim—like maybe they're trying to figure out a coverage limit or complaining about a specific client—my brain just completely latches onto their conversation. I can't tune it out. \n\nAnd it doesn't even have to be the office noise, honestly. Sometimes it's just my own head. I'll be right in the middle of a complex paragraph about property damage, and suddenly I'll remember that I need to pick up paper towels on the way home, or I'll wonder if I paid my electric bill, or I'll just start thinking about some random errand I have to run on the weekend. \n\nWhen that happens, it's like my attention just completely slides right off what I’m reading. My eyes are still moving over the words, you know? Like, I'm physically still looking at the document and scrolling. But nothing is actually going in. I'll get to the bottom of the page and realize I have absolutely no idea what I just looked at. So then I have to go back and reread the exact same section. And sometimes I have to reread it two, three, four times before the information actually sticks in my head, which just eats up so much time. It's so exhausting because it takes so much energy just to force my eyes to stay on the screen, and even then, if Brenda two desks down starts talking about her lunch, I'm suddenly fully invested in Brenda's lunch instead of the policy in front of me, and I have to start the whole rereading process all over again.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these difficulties with staying focused and sitting still? Was this something you struggled with back in school, or is this more of a recent problem that's developed over the past year or so?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because for a long time I really thought this was just a \"now\" problem, like maybe the open office or the boring claims were just finally getting to me. But when I really sit down and think about it... honestly, looking back, this definitely isn't brand new for me at all. Not even a little bit. \n\nI actually started thinking about this the other day, and I remembered how things were back when I was a kid. Like, way back in elementary school. I always felt like I was doing okay, you know, I wasn't failing or anything, but my parents kept all my old report cards in this big plastic bin in the basement, and I was looking through them a while ago. And it's crazy because every single year, it was the exact same story from every teacher. They would always write these little notes at the bottom, like, \"He's a very bright boy, very capable,\" but then the next sentence was always, always about how I was losing my worksheets. I mean, I would just shove papers into my desk or my backpack and they would just disappear into the void. \n\nAnd they also wrote about how I would constantly miss instructions. Like, the teacher would stand at the front of the room and explain exactly how to do the math assignment, and I would be looking right at her, but my brain was just... somewhere else entirely, probably thinking about recess or a cartoon or something. And then everyone would start working, and I'd just be sitting there completely lost, having to ask the kid next to me what we were supposed to be doing. \n\nThe teachers were always saying I needed constant reminders to actually finish my classwork, too. Because I'd start a worksheet, get maybe three problems in, and then I'd just start drawing in the margins or staring out the window, and the teacher would have to come tap on my desk and tell me to get back to work. So yeah, I guess when I really look at the whole picture, it's not just a recent thing at all. It's like I've always been this way, just kind of drifting off and losing track of things, even when I was just a little kid trying to get through third grade. It's just that now, instead of losing a spelling worksheet, I'm losing my place in a legal document, you know?\n\nDoctor: Besides the difficulty staying in your chair at work, have you noticed that you tend to be restless or fidgety in other situations too—like when you're watching TV at home, or sitting through a meeting, or even just trying to relax? Do your hands or legs tend to move around a lot?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah. Yeah, absolutely. It's... it's not just that I get up, it's that even when I am technically stuck in a chair, I'm never actually still. \n\nLike, we have these mandatory team meetings every Tuesday morning, and then sometimes they make us do these really long quarterly compliance trainings, right? They book out the big conference room on the second floor for those. And I go in there, and I always tell myself, \"Okay, just sit still, look professional, pretend you are locked in.\" Because I want to look focused, I really do. But man, within maybe five or ten minutes, my right leg is just going a mile a minute. I'll be bouncing my knee up and down under the table, completely unconsciously. Half the time I don't even realize I'm doing it until my knee bumps the underside of the table or I notice the water in somebody's glass vibrating a little bit. \n\nAnd then, you know, I'll catch myself and force my leg to stop. I'll press my foot flat on the floor and tell myself to just freeze. But the second I do that, the restlessness just moves somewhere else. I'll start shifting around in my chair endlessly. Those conference room chairs are those weird ergonomic mesh ones, and I just can't seem to find a position that feels right for more than two minutes. I'll cross my legs, then uncross them, lean to the left, sit back, lean forward on my elbows... I'm just constantly readjusting my weight. \n\nAnd the pens! If I have a pen in my hand—which I usually do, because we're supposed to be taking notes, even though they literally always just email us the PowerPoint deck afterwards anyway—I am just ruthlessly clicking it. Click, click, click, click. I actually had my supervisor, Dave, shoot me this really intense look across the table a couple of weeks ago because I was clicking my retractable pen over and over during a whole presentation on new underwriting guidelines. I felt terrible because I wasn't trying to be disruptive, my hands just needed something to do. \n\nSo yeah, even when I'm trying my absolute hardest to just look like a normal, attentive person in a meeting or a training, I'm bouncing, clicking, shifting... it's like the energy just has to leak out of me somehow. And it's the same at home, honestly. If I'm on the couch trying to watch a movie, I'm usually flipping my phone over and over in my hand or shaking my foot the entire time. I just can't seem to turn the motor off.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to your childhood—say, before you turned 12 years old—do you remember having these same kinds of problems with sitting still and paying attention, or did those issues really only start showing up once you were older?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, like I was just mentioning a minute ago when we were talking about those old report cards, it really wasn't a brand new thing for me at all. I mean, thinking specifically about before I was 12—so, you know, early elementary school—it was exactly that same stuff I was just talking about. \n\nLike I said, the teachers would always start off the report cards nice, writing that I was a \"bright boy\" or that I was \"very capable,\" but then it was just this laundry list of the exact same issues every single year. The losing worksheets thing was huge. I don't even know how I managed it, honestly, but my desk was just a complete black hole. I'd shove a paper in there and it would just get crushed under a million other things, just completely disappear. My mom used to get so frustrated because at parent-teacher conferences they'd always make me clean it out, and we'd find all these crumpled up, half-finished assignments from three weeks ago just stuffed in the back. \n\nAnd the missing instructions... yeah, that was definitely happening way back then, too. Like I was saying earlier, the teacher would be at the front of the room explaining a project or a math lesson, and I would be staring right at her, but my brain was just completely tuned out. I'd be watching a bug on the window or just daydreaming about whatever, and then suddenly the room gets loud and everyone is moving and opening their textbooks, and I'm just sitting there in a total panic because I have absolutely no idea what we're supposed to be doing or what page we're on. \n\nAnd then, of course, like I mentioned, they always had to give me constant reminders to actually finish my classwork. Because even if I did manage to figure out what to do, I'd do maybe one or two problems and then just start doodling in the margins or peeling the paper off my crayons. I had this one teacher in fourth grade, Mrs. Gable, who used to literally just walk by and tap on my desk with her knuckle every ten minutes. She wouldn't even say anything, just *tap tap*, because she knew if she didn't constantly pull my attention back to the paper, I would literally just stare at the wall until recess. So, yeah, it definitely goes way back before I was 12. It's pretty much always been there.\n\nDoctor: Looking back to those early school years—before you were 12—do you remember your parents or teachers ever expressing concern that these attention and restlessness issues were actually causing you problems with your grades, or getting into trouble, or keeping up with your schoolwork?\n\nPatient: Well, back then... I mean, they were definitely annoyed, and there were always those parent-teacher conferences where my mom would come home looking super stressed out because of the missing assignments and the messy desk. But the thing was, I wasn't really failing. I was smart enough that I could usually cram at the last second or guess my way through a test, so my grades were mostly okay—like, mostly Bs and Cs. So my parents and teachers were concerned about my potential, you know, always saying I wasn't \"living up to it\" or that I was being lazy, but it wasn't completely ruining my life back then. I wasn't getting suspended or held back or anything like that. I always just managed to scrape by.\n\nBut honestly, the reason I've been thinking about all this so much lately—and why I'm even sitting here talking to you today—is because of how much it's actually impacting my life *now*. Like, scraping by in fourth grade is one thing, but work... work is a totally different story, and it's completely falling apart on me. \n\nRemember how I mentioned my supervisor, Dave? The one who gave me the dirty look for clicking my pen? Well, it's gone way past just him being annoyed with me in meetings. He actually pulled me into his office last week for a formal one-on-one, and... well, he put me on an official performance coaching plan. Which is basically just HR corporate-speak for \"get it together right now or you're in major trouble.\" \n\nAnd it's all because my claim files are just completely falling behind. I have this huge queue of claims I'm supposed to be processing, and I just stare at the screen and can't make myself get through them fast enough, or I get distracted and lose my place and have to start reading the same file all over again. And worse than just being slow, Dave said my accuracy is being seriously questioned right now. I guess I've been missing key details in the policy documents, or putting the wrong codes into the system because my brain is just jumping all over the place and I'm rushing to catch up. \n\nAnd this is just... it's a total disaster for me, honestly. I've been at this company a while and I was really, really aiming for the senior adjuster track this year. It's a huge bump in pay, it's a better schedule, and I really wanted it. I thought I was next in line for it. But now, with this performance coaching thing on my record and my accuracy in the toilet, I am just terrified I'm going to lose that opportunity entirely. It just feels like my brain is literally costing me my career at this point, and I just... I can't seem to stop it from happening, no matter how hard I try to focus.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, did these attention and restlessness problems actually hold you back in any real way—like, did you fall behind your classmates academically, have trouble making friends, or get into trouble at school because of them? Or was it more just that your teachers were frustrated but you were still keeping up okay overall?\n\nPatient: Yeah, so, kind of like I was just saying a second ago, it was definitely more the second thing. I wasn't falling behind academically in any kind of permanent way. I mean, I never had to repeat a grade or go to summer school or anything drastic like that. \n\nLike I mentioned, I was getting mostly Bs and Cs because I could figure things out at the last possible second. I was basically the king of the panic-cram. Like, if we had a spelling test or a vocabulary quiz on a Friday, I wouldn't even look at the words Monday through Thursday. I'd lose the list, or forget it in my desk, and then Thursday night my mom would find out and be drilling me at the kitchen table while I was just completely overwhelmed and frustrated, but I'd still manage to pull off a B-minus or a C-plus the next morning. So on paper, I looked fine. I kept up okay, even if the process of getting there was a total mess behind the scenes. \n\nAnd as far as getting into trouble... I wasn't a bad kid at all. I wasn't getting sent to the principal's office for fighting or being defiant or anything. It was really just... exasperation. The teachers were just constantly exasperated with me. Like Mrs. Gable with her tapping on my desk. She wasn't furious with me, she was just tired of me staring at the window instead of my math sheet. And making friends was never really an issue either. I was pretty sociable—maybe a little too talkative when I was actually supposed to be reading quietly, which probably annoyed the teachers even more—but the other kids liked me fine. \n\nSo yeah, it didn't really hold me back back then. It was just a lot of friction, I guess you could call it. A lot of friction and annoyed adults, but no real, life-altering consequences. Not like now with Dave and the HR stuff. That's why it's all so jarring to me lately, because back then I could just skate by on being a \"bright boy\" who just needed to apply himself, but you can't really pull that off when you're 26 and messing up actual insurance claims, you know?\n\nDoctor: When you think back to those elementary school years, do you remember your parents or teachers ever saying that these attention and restlessness problems were actually causing real problems for you—like, was it affecting your ability to do things you wanted to do, or causing you problems at home or with other kids, or keeping you from activities you cared about?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it really is just like I was saying a minute ago. I mean, I don't think they ever felt like it was ruining my life or anything like that. Because, you know, when it came to things I actually *wanted* to do? I was totally fine. Like, it never kept me from my activities or anything. If anything, my mom would get more annoyed because she’d see me out in the yard building this incredibly elaborate fort out of cardboard boxes for three hours straight, completely focused, or I'd be playing video games until dinner, and then she’d be like, \"Why can't you just do that with your spelling worksheet?\" So it definitely wasn't stopping me from having fun or doing the stuff I cared about. \n\nAnd at home... I mean, yeah, like I said, my mom was stressed about the messy desk and those Thursday night cram sessions, but it wasn't causing, like, massive family blowouts. I wasn't getting grounded for weeks on end or anything. It was just this constant, low-level annoyance for her. She'd sigh a lot, you know? Like, \"Here we go again with the missing paper.\" But we still had a totally normal family life otherwise. We went on camping trips, we watched movies together. It wasn't this dark cloud hanging over the whole house. My dad worked a lot, so he wasn't as involved in the day-to-day homework battles, but even he just kind of saw it as me being a bit scatterbrained. \n\nAnd with other kids, like I mentioned, I was always pretty social. I had my group of friends, we rode bikes around the neighborhood, traded cards, all that normal kid stuff. None of them cared if I lost my math homework or if Mrs. Gable had to tap on my desk. Kids don't really pay attention to that stuff unless you're, like, throwing chairs or something, which I never did. \n\nSo, no, I really don't remember anyone saying it was causing *real* damage to my life back then. It was just... I was just the disorganized kid who daydreamed too much. That was my label. It's just so crazy to think about how different it is now, because back then the worst thing that happened was my mom giving me a lecture at the kitchen table, and now I'm literally sitting in HR meetings sweating through my shirt because Dave is looking at my claim processing times and telling me I'm missing policy details. It's just... it's a completely different world now. When you're a kid, people just kind of cover for you or push you along, but nobody is doing that for me at the insurance firm.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I can't seem to stop it from happening, no matter how hard I try to focus.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, did these attention and restlessness problems actually hold you back in any real way—like, did you fall behind your classmates academically, have trouble making friends, or get into trouble at school because of them? Or was it more just that your teachers were frustrated but you were still keeping up okay overall?\n\nPatient: Yeah, so, kind of like I was just saying a second ago, it was definitely more the second thing. I wasn't falling behind academically in any kind of permanent way. I mean, I never had to repeat a grade or go to summer school or anything drastic like that. \n\nLike I mentioned, I was getting mostly Bs and Cs because I could figure things out at the last possible second. I was basically the king of the panic-cram. Like, if we had a spelling test or a vocabulary quiz on a Friday, I wouldn't even look at the words Monday through Thursday. I'd lose the list, or forget it in my desk, and then Thursday night my mom would find out and be drilling me at the kitchen table while I was just completely overwhelmed and frustrated, but I'd still manage to pull off a B-minus or a C-plus the next morning. So on paper, I looked fine. I kept up okay, even if the process of getting there was a total mess behind the scenes. \n\nAnd as far as getting into trouble... I wasn't a bad kid at all. I wasn't getting sent to the principal's office for fighting or being defiant or anything. It was really just... exasperation. The teachers were just constantly exasperated with me. Like Mrs. Gable with her tapping on my desk. She wasn't furious with me, she was just tired of me staring at the window instead of my math sheet. And making friends was never really an issue either. I was pretty sociable—maybe a little too talkative when I was actually supposed to be reading quietly, which probably annoyed the teachers even more—but the other kids liked me fine. \n\nSo yeah, it didn't really hold me back back then. It was just a lot of friction, I guess you could call it. A lot of friction and annoyed adults, but no real, life-altering consequences. Not like now with Dave and the HR stuff. That's why it's all so jarring to me lately, because back then I could just skate by on being a \"bright boy\" who just needed to apply himself, but you can't really pull that off when you're 26 and messing up actual insurance claims, you know?\n\nDoctor: When you think back to those elementary school years, do you remember your parents or teachers ever saying that these attention and restlessness problems were actually causing real problems for you—like, was it affecting your ability to do things you wanted to do, or causing you problems at home or with other kids, or keeping you from activities you cared about?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it really is just like I was saying a minute ago. I mean, I don't think they ever felt like it was ruining my life or anything like that. Because, you know, when it came to things I actually *wanted* to do? I was totally fine. Like, it never kept me from my activities or anything. If anything, my mom would get more annoyed because she’d see me out in the yard building this incredibly elaborate fort out of cardboard boxes for three hours straight, completely focused, or I'd be playing video games until dinner, and then she’d be like, \"Why can't you just do that with your spelling worksheet?\" So it definitely wasn't stopping me from having fun or doing the stuff I cared about. \n\nAnd at home... I mean, yeah, like I said, my mom was stressed about the messy desk and those Thursday night cram sessions, but it wasn't causing, like, massive family blowouts. I wasn't getting grounded for weeks on end or anything. It was just this constant, low-level annoyance for her. She'd sigh a lot, you know? Like, \"Here we go again with the missing paper.\" But we still had a totally normal family life otherwise. We went on camping trips, we watched movies together. It wasn't this dark cloud hanging over the whole house. My dad worked a lot, so he wasn't as involved in the day-to-day homework battles, but even he just kind of saw it as me being a bit scatterbrained. \n\nAnd with other kids, like I mentioned, I was always pretty social. I had my group of friends, we rode bikes around the neighborhood, traded cards, all that normal kid stuff. None of them cared if I lost my math homework or if Mrs. Gable had to tap on my desk. Kids don't really pay attention to that stuff unless you're, like, throwing chairs or something, which I never did. \n\nSo, no, I really don't remember anyone saying it was causing *real* damage to my life back then. It was just... I was just the disorganized kid who daydreamed too much. That was my label. It's just so crazy to think about how different it is now, because back then the worst thing that happened was my mom giving me a lecture at the kitchen table, and now I'm literally sitting in HR meetings sweating through my shirt because Dave is looking at my claim processing times and telling me I'm missing policy details. It's just... it's a completely different world now. When you're a kid, people just kind of cover for you or push you along, but nobody is doing that for me at the insurance firm.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Honestly, no. I know it probably sounds like I’m just repeating myself, or maybe like I’m downplaying it, but I promise I’m really not. It just truly never got to that level of actual, serious conflict. 

As far as missing out on things... no, never. My parents weren't really the strict, authoritarian types, you know? They wouldn't ground me from hanging out with my friends or take away my weekends just because my desk was a disaster area or I forgot my vocabulary words. I mean, maybe once in a blue moon I had to stay inside for an extra fifteen minutes during recess to finish a worksheet I was supposed to do during class, but that was really the absolute worst of it. It never kept me from doing the things I actually cared about. 

And with my parents and teachers, it was never an adversarial thing. There were no screaming matches at the dinner table. My mom never yelled, she just... she'd just sit at the kitchen table rubbing her temples, looking completely drained. And my teachers, like Mrs. Gable or my fifth-grade teacher, they never yelled either. They just looked at me with this sort of mix of pity and total exhaustion. 

So there really weren't any lasting consequences back then. It's not like I have this deep childhood trauma of being severely punished for not paying attention. The consequences were entirely... I don't know, invisible, I guess? It was mostly just me feeling kind of stupid sometimes when I'd suddenly realize everyone else in the room knew exactly what page we were on and I was still staring out the window at the parking lot. But no one was screaming at me, and it didn't ruin my relationships.

And I think... I think that's exactly why this whole situation with Dave at work is hitting me like a ton of bricks right now. I don't have any experience with actual, real-world consequences for this stuff. When you're nine years old, people just give you a pass. They sigh, they tap your desk, they help you find your missing paper, and life goes on. But when you're twenty-six and you're processing insurance claims that affect people's actual money and the company's bottom line... there are no passes. Dave isn't going to just sigh and help me find my place in the policy document. He's going to fire me. And that's just terrifying, because I feel like I'm trying just as hard to focus now as I was back then, but the safety net is completely gone and I'm just in freefall.

Was this something you struggled with back in school, or is this more of a recent problem that's developed over the past year or so?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because for a long time I really thought this was just a \"now\" problem, like maybe the open office or the boring claims were just finally getting to me. But when I really sit down and think about it... honestly, looking back, this definitely isn't brand new for me at all. I always felt like I was doing okay, you know, I wasn't failing or anything, but my parents kept all my old report cards in this big plastic bin in the basement, and I was looking through them a while ago. They would always write these little notes at the bottom, like, \"He's a very bright boy, very capable,\" but then the next sentence was always, always about how I was losing my worksheets. So yeah, I guess when I really look at the whole picture, it's not just a recent thing at all. It's like I've always been this way, just kind of drifting off and losing track of things, even when I was just a little kid trying to get through third grade. It's just that now, instead of losing a spelling worksheet, I'm losing my place in a legal document, you know?\n\nDoctor: Besides the difficulty staying in your chair at work, have you noticed that you tend to be restless or fidgety in other situations too—like when you're watching TV at home, or sitting through a meeting, or even just trying to relax? Do your hands or legs tend to move around a lot?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah. Yeah, absolutely. It's... it's not just that I get up, it's that even when I am technically stuck in a chair, I'm never actually still. \n\nLike, we have these mandatory team meetings every Tuesday morning, and then sometimes they make us do these really long quarterly compliance trainings, right? They book out the big conference room on the second floor for those. And I go in there, and I always tell myself, \"Okay, just sit still, look professional, pretend you are locked in.\" Because I want to look focused, I really do. But man, within maybe five or ten minutes, my right leg is just going a mile a minute. I'll be bouncing my knee up and down under the table, completely unconsciously. Half the time I don't even realize I'm doing it until my knee bumps the underside of the table or I notice the water in somebody's glass vibrating a little bit. \n\nAnd then, you know, I'll catch myself and force my leg to stop. I'll press my foot flat on the floor and tell myself to just freeze. But the second I do that, the restlessness just moves somewhere else. I'll start shifting around in my chair endlessly. Those conference room chairs are those weird ergonomic mesh ones, and I just can't seem to find a position that feels right for more than two minutes. I'll cross my legs, then uncross them, lean to the left, sit back, lean forward on my elbows... I'm just constantly readjusting my weight. \n\nAnd the pens! And it's the same at home, honestly. If I'm on the couch trying to watch a movie, I'm usually flipping my phone over and over in my hand or shaking my foot the entire time. I just can't seem to turn the motor off.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to your childhood—say, before you turned 12 years old—do you remember having these same kinds of problems with sitting still and paying attention, or did those issues really only start showing up once you were older?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, like I was just mentioning a minute ago when we were talking about those old report cards, it really wasn't a brand new thing for me at all. I thought I was next in line for it. But now, with this performance coaching thing on my record and my accuracy in the toilet, I am just terrified I'm going to lose that opportunity entirely. It just feels like my brain is literally costing me my career at this point, and I just... I can't seem to stop it from happening, no matter how hard I try to focus.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, did these attention and restlessness problems actually hold you back in any real way—like, did you fall behind your classmates academically, have trouble making friends, or get into trouble at school because of them? Or was it more just that your teachers were frustrated but you were still keeping up okay overall?\n\nPatient: Yeah, so, kind of like I was just saying a second ago, it was definitely more the second thing. I wasn't falling behind academically in any kind of permanent way. I mean, I never had to repeat a grade or go to summer school or anything drastic like that. \n\nLike I mentioned, I was getting mostly Bs and Cs because I could figure things out at the last possible second. I was basically the king of the panic-cram. Like, if we had a spelling test or a vocabulary quiz on a Friday, I wouldn't even look at the words Monday through Thursday. I'd lose the list, or forget it in my desk, and then Thursday night my mom would find out and be drilling me at the kitchen table while I was just completely overwhelmed and frustrated, but I'd still manage to pull off a B-minus or a C-plus the next morning. I was pretty sociable—maybe a little too talkative when I was actually supposed to be reading quietly, which probably annoyed the teachers even more—but the other kids liked me fine. \n\nSo yeah, it didn't really hold me back back then. It was just a lot of friction, I guess you could call it. A lot of friction and annoyed adults, but no real, life-altering consequences. Not like now with Dave and the HR stuff. That's why it's all so jarring to me lately, because back then I could just skate by on being a \"bright boy\" who just needed to apply himself, but you can't really pull that off when you're 26 and messing up actual insurance claims, you know?\n\nDoctor: When you think back to those elementary school years, do you remember your parents or teachers ever saying that these attention and restlessness problems were actually causing real problems for you—like, was it affecting your ability to do things you wanted to do, or causing you problems at home or with other kids, or keeping you from activities you cared about?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it really is just like I was saying a minute ago. I mean, I don't think they ever felt like it was ruining my life or anything like that. Because, you know, when it came to things I actually *wanted* to do? I was totally fine. Like, it never kept me from my activities or anything. I mean, yeah, like I said, my mom was stressed about the messy desk and those Thursday night cram sessions, but it wasn't causing, like, massive family blowouts. I wasn't getting grounded for weeks on end or anything. It was just this constant, low-level annoyance for her. She'd sigh a lot, you know? Like, \"Here we go again with the missing paper.\" But we still had a totally normal family life otherwise. We went on camping trips, we watched movies together. It wasn't this dark cloud hanging over the whole house. My dad worked a lot, so he wasn't as involved in the day-to-day homework battles, but even he just kind of saw it as me being a bit scatterbrained. \n\nAnd with other kids, like I mentioned, I was always pretty social. I had my group of friends, we rode bikes around the neighborhood, traded cards, all that normal kid stuff. None of them cared if I lost my math homework or if Mrs. Gable had to tap on my desk. Kids don't really pay attention to that stuff unless you're, like, throwing chairs or something, which I never did. \n\nSo, no, I really don't remember anyone saying it was causing *real* damage to my life back then. It was just... I was just the disorganized kid who daydreamed too much. That was my label. It's just so crazy to think about how different it is now, because back then the worst thing that happened was my mom giving me a lecture at the kitchen table, and now I'm literally sitting in HR meetings sweating through my shirt because Dave is looking at my claim processing times and telling me I'm missing policy details. It's just... it's a completely different world now. When you're a kid, people just kind of cover for you or push you along, but nobody is doing that for me at the insurance firm.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that back in elementary school, you were mostly getting Bs and Cs and keeping up okay overall. I know it probably sounds like I’m just repeating myself, or maybe like I’m downplaying it, but I promise I’m really not. It just truly never got to that level of actual, serious conflict. \n\nAs far as missing out on things... no, never. My parents weren't really the strict, authoritarian types, you know? They wouldn't ground me from hanging out with my friends or take away my weekends just because my desk was a disaster area or I forgot my vocabulary words. I mean, maybe once in a blue moon I had to stay inside for an extra fifteen minutes during recess to finish a worksheet I was supposed to do during class, but that was really the absolute worst of it. It never kept me from doing the things I actually cared about. \n\nAnd with my parents and teachers, it was never an adversarial thing. There were no screaming matches at the dinner table. My mom never yelled, she just... she'd just sit at the kitchen table rubbing her temples, looking completely drained. They just looked at me with this sort of mix of pity and total exhaustion. \n\nSo there really weren't any lasting consequences back then. I'll press my foot flat on the floor and tell myself to just freeze. But the second I do that, the restlessness just moves somewhere else. I'll start shifting around in my chair endlessly. Those conference room chairs are those weird ergonomic mesh ones, and I just can't seem to find a position that feels right for more than two minutes. I'll cross my legs, then uncross them, lean to the left, sit back, lean forward on my elbows... I'm just constantly readjusting my weight. \n\nAnd the pens! If I have a pen in my hand—which I usually do, because we're supposed to be taking notes, even though they literally always just email us the PowerPoint deck afterwards anyway—I am just ruthlessly clicking it. Click, click, click, click. I actually had my supervisor, Dave, shoot me this really intense look across the table a couple of weeks ago because I was clicking my retractable pen over and over during a whole presentation on new underwriting guidelines. I felt terrible because I wasn't trying to be disruptive, my hands just needed something to do. \n\nSo yeah, even when I'm trying my absolute hardest to just look like a normal, attentive person in a meeting or a training, I'm bouncing, clicking, shifting... it's like the energy just has to leak out of me somehow. And it's the same at home, honestly. If I'm on the couch trying to watch a movie, I'm usually flipping my phone over and over in my hand or shaking my foot the entire time. I just can't seem to turn the motor off.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to your childhood—say, before you turned 12 years old—do you remember having these same kinds of problems with sitting still and paying attention, or did those issues really only start showing up once you were older?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, like I was just mentioning a minute ago when we were talking about those old report cards, it really wasn't a brand new thing for me at all. I mean, thinking specifically about before I was 12—so, you know, early elementary school—it was exactly that same stuff I was just talking about. \n\nLike I said, the teachers would always start off the report cards nice, writing that I was a \"bright boy\" or that I was \"very capable,\" but then it was just this laundry list of the exact same issues every single year. The losing worksheets thing was huge. I don't even know how I managed it, honestly, but my desk was just a complete black hole. I'd shove a paper in there and it would just get crushed under a million other things, just completely disappear. My mom used to get so frustrated because at parent-teacher conferences they'd always make me clean it out, and we'd find all these crumpled up, half-finished assignments from three weeks ago just stuffed in the back. \n\nAnd the missing instructions... yeah, that was definitely happening way back then, too. Like I was saying earlier, the teacher would be at the front of the room explaining a project or a math lesson, and I would be staring right at her, but my brain was just completely tuned out. I'd be watching a bug on the window or just daydreaming about whatever, and then suddenly the room gets loud and everyone is moving and opening their textbooks, and I'm just sitting there in a total panic because I have absolutely no idea what we're supposed to be doing or what page we're on. \n\nAnd then, of course, like I mentioned, they always had to give me constant reminders to actually finish my classwork. Because even if I did manage to figure out what to do, I'd do maybe one or two problems and then just start doodling in the margins or peeling the paper off my crayons. I had this one teacher in fourth grade, Mrs. Gable, who used to literally just walk by and tap on my desk with her knuckle every ten minutes. She wouldn't even say anything, just *tap tap*, because she knew if she didn't constantly pull my attention back to the paper, I would literally just stare at the wall until recess. So, yeah, it definitely goes way back before I was 12. It's pretty much always been there.\n\nDoctor: Looking back to those early school years—before you were 12—do you remember your parents or teachers ever expressing concern that these attention and restlessness issues were actually causing you problems with your grades, or getting into trouble, or keeping up with your schoolwork?\n\nPatient: Well, back then... I mean, they were definitely annoyed, and there were always those parent-teacher conferences where my mom would come home looking super stressed out because of the missing assignments and the messy desk. But the thing was, I wasn't really failing. I was smart enough that I could usually cram at the last second or guess my way through a test, so my grades were mostly okay—like, mostly Bs and Cs. So my parents and teachers were concerned about my potential, you know, always saying I wasn't \"living up to it\" or that I was being lazy, but it wasn't completely ruining my life back then. I wasn't getting suspended or held back or anything like that. I always just managed to scrape by.\n\nBut honestly, the reason I've been thinking about all this so much lately—and why I'm even sitting here talking to you today—is because of how much it's actually impacting my life *now*. Like, scraping by in fourth grade is one thing, but work... work is a totally different story, and it's completely falling apart on me. \n\nRemember how I mentioned my supervisor, Dave? The one who gave me the dirty look for clicking my pen? Well, it's gone way past just him being annoyed with me in meetings. He actually pulled me into his office last week for a formal one-on-one, and... well, he put me on an official performance coaching plan. Which is basically just HR corporate-speak for \"get it together right now or you're in major trouble.\" \n\nAnd it's all because my claim files are just completely falling behind. I have this huge queue of claims I'm supposed to be processing, and I just stare at the screen and can't make myself get through them fast enough, or I get distracted and lose my place and have to start reading the same file all over again. And worse than just being slow, Dave said my accuracy is being seriously questioned right now. I guess I've been missing key details in the policy documents, or putting the wrong codes into the system because my brain is just jumping all over the place and I'm rushing to catch up. \n\nAnd this is just... it's a total disaster for me, honestly. I've been at this company a while and I was really, really aiming for the senior adjuster track this year. It's a huge bump in pay, it's a better schedule, and I really wanted it. I thought I was next in line for it. But now, with this performance coaching thing on my record and my accuracy in the toilet, I am just terrified I'm going to lose that opportunity entirely. It just feels like my brain is literally costing me my career at this point, and I just... I can't seem to stop it from happening, no matter how hard I try to focus.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, did these attention and restlessness problems actually hold you back in any real way—like, did you fall behind your classmates academically, have trouble making friends, or get into trouble at school because of them? Or was it more just that your teachers were frustrated but you were still keeping up okay overall?\n\nPatient: Yeah, so, kind of like I was just saying a second ago, it was definitely more the second thing. I wasn't falling behind academically in any kind of permanent way. I mean, I never had to repeat a grade or go to summer school or anything drastic like that. \n\nLike I mentioned, I was getting mostly Bs and Cs because I could figure things out at the last possible second. I was basically the king of the panic-cram. Like, if we had a spelling test or a vocabulary quiz on a Friday, I wouldn't even look at the words Monday through Thursday. I'd lose the list, or forget it in my desk, and then Thursday night my mom would find out and be drilling me at the kitchen table while I was just completely overwhelmed and frustrated, but I'd still manage to pull off a B-minus or a C-plus the next morning. So on paper, I looked fine. I kept up okay, even if the process of getting there was a total mess behind the scenes. \n\nAnd as far as getting into trouble... I wasn't a bad kid at all. I wasn't getting sent to the principal's office for fighting or being defiant or anything. It was really just... exasperation. The teachers were just constantly exasperated with me. Like Mrs. Gable with her tapping on my desk. She wasn't furious with me, she was just tired of me staring at the window instead of my math sheet. And making friends was never really an issue either. I was pretty sociable—maybe a little too talkative when I was actually supposed to be reading quietly, which probably annoyed the teachers even more—but the other kids liked me fine. \n\nSo yeah, it didn't really hold me back back then. It was just a lot of friction, I guess you could call it. A lot of friction and annoyed adults, but no real, life-altering consequences. Not like now with Dave and the HR stuff. That's why it's all so jarring to me lately, because back then I could just skate by on being a \"bright boy\" who just needed to apply himself, but you can't really pull that off when you're 26 and messing up actual insurance claims, you know?\n\nDoctor: When you think back to those elementary school years, do you remember your parents or teachers ever saying that these attention and restlessness problems were actually causing real problems for you—like, was it affecting your ability to do things you wanted to do, or causing you problems at home or with other kids, or keeping you from activities you cared about?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it really is just like I was saying a minute ago. I mean, I don't think they ever felt like it was ruining my life or anything like that. Because, you know, when it came to things I actually *wanted* to do? I was totally fine. Like, it never kept me from my activities or anything. If anything, my mom would get more annoyed because she’d see me out in the yard building this incredibly elaborate fort out of cardboard boxes for three hours straight, completely focused, or I'd be playing video games until dinner, and then she’d be like, \"Why can't you just do that with your spelling worksheet?\" So it definitely wasn't stopping me from having fun or doing the stuff I cared about. \n\nAnd at home... I mean, yeah, like I said, my mom was stressed about the messy desk and those Thursday night cram sessions, but it wasn't causing, like, massive family blowouts. I wasn't getting grounded for weeks on end or anything. It was just this constant, low-level annoyance for her. She'd sigh a lot, you know? Like, \"Here we go again with the missing paper.\" But we still had a totally normal family life otherwise. We went on camping trips, we watched movies together. It wasn't this dark cloud hanging over the whole house. My dad worked a lot, so he wasn't as involved in the day-to-day homework battles, but even he just kind of saw it as me being a bit scatterbrained. \n\nAnd with other kids, like I mentioned, I was always pretty social. I had my group of friends, we rode bikes around the neighborhood, traded cards, all that normal kid stuff. None of them cared if I lost my math homework or if Mrs. Gable had to tap on my desk. Kids don't really pay attention to that stuff unless you're, like, throwing chairs or something, which I never did. \n\nSo, no, I really don't remember anyone saying it was causing *real* damage to my life back then. It was just... I was just the disorganized kid who daydreamed too much. That was my label. It's just so crazy to think about how different it is now, because back then the worst thing that happened was my mom giving me a lecture at the kitchen table, and now I'm literally sitting in HR meetings sweating through my shirt because Dave is looking at my claim processing times and telling me I'm missing policy details. It's just... it's a completely different world now. When you're a kid, people just kind of cover for you or push you along, but nobody is doing that for me at the insurance firm.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that back in elementary school, you were mostly getting Bs and Cs and keeping up okay overall. But I want to ask you more specifically: did these attention and restlessness problems ever cause you to actually miss out on things that mattered to you, or cause real problems between you and your parents or teachers—not just frustration, but actual conflict or consequences that stuck with you?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no. I know it probably sounds like I’m just repeating myself, or maybe like I’m downplaying it, but I promise I’m really not. It just truly never got to that level of actual, serious conflict. \n\nAs far as missing out on things... no, never. My parents weren't really the strict, authoritarian types, you know? They wouldn't ground me from hanging out with my friends or take away my weekends just because my desk was a disaster area or I forgot my vocabulary words. I mean, maybe once in a blue moon I had to stay inside for an extra fifteen minutes during recess to finish a worksheet I was supposed to do during class, but that was really the absolute worst of it. It never kept me from doing the things I actually cared about. \n\nAnd with my parents and teachers, it was never an adversarial thing. There were no screaming matches at the dinner table. My mom never yelled, she just... she'd just sit at the kitchen table rubbing her temples, looking completely drained. And my teachers, like Mrs. Gable or my fifth-grade teacher, they never yelled either. They just looked at me with this sort of mix of pity and total exhaustion. \n\nSo there really weren't any lasting consequences back then. It's not like I have this deep childhood trauma of being severely punished for not paying attention. The consequences were entirely... I don't know, invisible, I guess? It was mostly just me feeling kind of stupid sometimes when I'd suddenly realize everyone else in the room knew exactly what page we were on and I was still staring out the window at the parking lot. But no one was screaming at me, and it didn't ruin my relationships.\n\nAnd I think... I think that's exactly why this whole situation with Dave at work is hitting me like a ton of bricks right now. I don't have any experience with actual, real-world consequences for this stuff. When you're nine years old, people just give you a pass. They sigh, they tap your desk, they help you find your missing paper, and life goes on. But when you're twenty-six and you're processing insurance claims that affect people's actual money and the company's bottom line... there are no passes. Dave isn't going to just sigh and help me find my place in the policy document. He's going to fire me. And that's just terrifying, because I feel like I'm trying just as hard to focus now as I was back then, but the safety net is completely gone and I'm just in freefall.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

If anything, my mom would get more annoyed because she’d see me out in the yard building this incredibly elaborate fort out of cardboard boxes for three hours straight, completely focused, or I'd be playing video games until dinner, and then she’d be like, \"Why can't you just do that with your spelling worksheet?\" So it definitely wasn't stopping me from having fun or doing the stuff I cared about. \n\nAnd at home... I mean, yeah, like I said, my mom was stressed about the messy desk and those Thursday night cram sessions, but it wasn't causing, like, massive family blowouts. I wasn't getting grounded for weeks on end or anything. It was just this constant, low-level annoyance for her. She'd sigh a lot, you know? Like, \"Here we go again with the missing paper.\" But we still had a totally normal family life otherwise. We went on camping trips, we watched movies together. It wasn't this dark cloud hanging over the whole house. My dad worked a lot, so he wasn't as involved in the day-to-day homework battles, but even he just kind of saw it as me being a bit scatterbrained. \n\nAnd with other kids, like I mentioned, I was always pretty social. I had my group of friends, we rode bikes around the neighborhood, traded cards, all that normal kid stuff. None of them cared if I lost my math homework or if Mrs. Gable had to tap on my desk. Kids don't really pay attention to that stuff unless you're, like, throwing chairs or something, which I never did. \n\nSo, no, I really don't remember anyone saying it was causing *real* damage to my life back then. It was just... I was just the disorganized kid who daydreamed too much. That was my label. It's just so crazy to think about how different it is now, because back then the worst thing that happened was my mom giving me a lecture at the kitchen table, and now I'm literally sitting in HR meetings sweating through my shirt because Dave is looking at my claim processing times and telling me I'm missing policy details. It's just... it's a completely different world now. When you're a kid, people just kind of cover for you or push you along, but nobody is doing that for me at the insurance firm.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that back in elementary school, you were mostly getting Bs and Cs and keeping up okay overall. But I want to ask you more specifically: did these attention and restlessness problems ever cause you to actually miss out on things that mattered to you, or cause real problems between you and your parents or teachers—not just frustration, but actual conflict or consequences that stuck with you?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no. I know it probably sounds like I’m just repeating myself, or maybe like I’m downplaying it, but I promise I’m really not. It just truly never got to that level of actual, serious conflict. \n\nAs far as missing out on things... no, never. My parents weren't really the strict, authoritarian types, you know? They wouldn't ground me from hanging out with my friends or take away my weekends just because my desk was a disaster area or I forgot my vocabulary words. I mean, maybe once in a blue moon I had to stay inside for an extra fifteen minutes during recess to finish a worksheet I was supposed to do during class, but that was really the absolute worst of it. It never kept me from doing the things I actually cared about. \n\nAnd with my parents and teachers, it was never an adversarial thing. There were no screaming matches at the dinner table. My mom never yelled, she just... she'd just sit at the kitchen table rubbing her temples, looking completely drained. And my teachers, like Mrs. Gable or my fifth-grade teacher, they never yelled either. They just looked at me with this sort of mix of pity and total exhaustion. \n\nSo there really weren't any lasting consequences back then. It's not like I have this deep childhood trauma of being severely punished for not paying attention. The consequences were entirely... I don't know, invisible, I guess? It was mostly just me feeling kind of stupid sometimes when I'd suddenly realize everyone else in the room knew exactly what page we were on and I was still staring out the window at the parking lot. But no one was screaming at me, and it didn't ruin my relationships.\n\nAnd I think... I think that's exactly why this whole situation with Dave at work is hitting me like a ton of bricks right now. I don't have any experience with actual, real-world consequences for this stuff. When you're nine years old, people just give you a pass. They sigh, they tap your desk, they help you find your missing paper, and life goes on. But when you're twenty-six and you're processing insurance claims that affect people's actual money and the company's bottom line... there are no passes. Dave isn't going to just sigh and help me find my place in the policy document. He's going to fire me. And that's just terrifying, because I feel like I'm trying just as hard to focus now as I was back then, but the safety net is completely gone and I'm just in freefall.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you think back to elementary school, do you remember your parents or teachers ever saying that your attention or restlessness problems were actually making it hard for you to do your schoolwork, or that they were concerned enough about it to want you to see a doctor or get some kind of help?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 26 / insurance_claims_or_policy_processing_clerk / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.
