========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean... honestly? If I really think about it, I definitely wasn't performing at the level I was capable of. I was totally underperforming, even if it didn't look like it on paper because I was getting those Bs and Cs. Because, like I said, I was bright enough to just sort of coast. 

But looking back at it now, with everything going on at work with Dave and the insurance policies... it's like this giant lightbulb just went off, you know? Because this feeling of just barely skating by, of not living up to my potential... this wasn't brand new for me. It really wasn't. Like, I remember—and this is so clear now that I'm actually thinking about it—I remember my mom keeping all my report cards in this big accordion folder in the bottom drawer of the filing cabinet in the den. And every single year, from like second grade all the way through fifth, the teachers would write the exact same kind of comments in that little box at the bottom. 

It was always some variation of, "He is a very bright boy, but..." And then the "but" was always this laundry list of things that are literally exactly what I'm doing now at work. They'd write that I was bright, but I constantly lost my worksheets. Or that I was smart, but I always missed the verbal instructions when she was standing right at the front of the room talking to us. Or they'd say I needed constant reminders just to finish basic classwork that everyone else was already done with. 

Like, I remember Mrs. Gable—I keep bringing her up, but she was my fourth-grade teacher and she was so strict—she actually wrote in red pen once, "He has so much potential if he could just apply himself and keep track of his assignments." And my mom was so annoyed, she made me sit at the kitchen table and reorganize my entire Trapper Keeper, which took like three hours because I had shoved so many loose, half-finished math sheets into the pockets that they were all ripped and crinkled. 

So, yeah. I was smart enough to pass, and I guess that's why no one sounded the alarm. But I definitely wasn't doing what I could have been doing if I had just been able to focus and keep track of my stuff like a normal kid. And it's just wild to me that I'm sitting here at twenty-six, and my boss is essentially telling me the exact same thing Mrs. Gable told my mom when I was ten. It's like I've been stuck in this same loop my entire life, just losing papers and missing instructions, and I never even realized it until my job was on the line.

Like I mentioned before, I was mostly getting Bs and Cs, so I wasn't failing anything. It really didn't get in the way of my actual grades or stop me from moving up to the next grade level with all my friends. I was never in remedial classes or anything like that. It's just... it's a completely different world now. When you're a kid, people just kind of cover for you or push you along, but nobody is doing that for me at the insurance firm.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that back in elementary school, you were mostly getting Bs and Cs and keeping up okay overall. But I want to ask you more specifically: did these attention and restlessness problems ever cause you to actually miss out on things that mattered to you, or cause real problems between you and your parents or teachers—not just frustration, but actual conflict or consequences that stuck with you?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no. I know it probably sounds like I’m just repeating myself, or maybe like I’m downplaying it, but I promise I’m really not. It just truly never got to that level of actual, serious conflict. \n\nAs far as missing out on things... no, never. My parents weren't really the strict, authoritarian types, you know? They wouldn't ground me from hanging out with my friends or take away my weekends just because my desk was a disaster area or I forgot my vocabulary words. I mean, maybe once in a blue moon I had to stay inside for an extra fifteen minutes during recess to finish a worksheet I was supposed to do during class, but that was really the absolute worst of it. It never kept me from doing the things I actually cared about. \n\nAnd with my parents and teachers, it was never an adversarial thing. There were no screaming matches at the dinner table. My mom never yelled, she just... she'd just sit at the kitchen table rubbing her temples, looking completely drained. And my teachers, like Mrs. Gable or my fifth-grade teacher, they never yelled either. They just looked at me with this sort of mix of pity and total exhaustion. \n\nSo there really weren't any lasting consequences back then. It's not like I have this deep childhood trauma of being severely punished for not paying attention. The consequences were entirely... I don't know, invisible, I guess? It was mostly just me feeling kind of stupid sometimes when I'd suddenly realize everyone else in the room knew exactly what page we were on and I was still staring out the window at the parking lot. But no one was screaming at me, and it didn't ruin my relationships.\n\nAnd I think... I think that's exactly why this whole situation with Dave at work is hitting me like a ton of bricks right now. I don't have any experience with actual, real-world consequences for this stuff. When you're nine years old, people just give you a pass. They sigh, they tap your desk, they help you find your missing paper, and life goes on. But when you're twenty-six and you're processing insurance claims that affect people's actual money and the company's bottom line... there are no passes. Dave isn't going to just sigh and help me find my place in the policy document. He's going to fire me. And that's just terrifying, because I feel like I'm trying just as hard to focus now as I was back then, but the safety net is completely gone and I'm just in freefall.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to elementary school, do you remember your parents or teachers ever saying that your attention or restlessness problems were actually making it hard for you to do your schoolwork, or that they were concerned enough about it to want you to see a doctor or get some kind of help?\n\nPatient: I feel like a broken record here, but honestly, no. It really never crossed that line for anyone. \n\nLike I was saying about the Thursday night cramming, they definitely knew the homework was a battle, but they never said it was making it impossible for me to *do* the work, if that makes sense. Because I *could* do it, eventually. My mom would always use that exact phrase—\"He has the brains, he just doesn't apply himself.\" That was the big takeaway. I just needed to apply myself more. \n\nAnd as for seeing a doctor or getting some kind of professional help? No way. That never even came up. Not once. I don't know if it was just the school I was at or how things were back then, but nobody was jumping to send a kid to a doctor just because his desk looked like a paper recycling bin had exploded inside it. They just viewed it as a discipline thing, or an organizational issue. \n\nMy mom's solution was never a doctor. Her solution was Office Depot. Every single August, right before school started, we'd go and buy these incredibly elaborate planner systems. She'd get me those big Trapper Keepers with the different colored folders—like, red was always for math, blue for science, green for language arts. And she'd buy these special daily assignment notebooks where I was supposed to write down every single thing the teacher wrote on the chalkboard. She really thought that if I just had the right binder, I'd magically become this perfectly organized, focused student. \n\nAnd, I mean, of course, by the second week of October, the folders were ripped, half my worksheets were just shoved loose into the bottom of my backpack, and the planner was completely lost. But her reaction to that was never, \"Oh, maybe there's an underlying issue here, let's call the pediatrician.\" It was just, \"Why aren't you using the system we bought? Why are you being so careless?\" \n\nSo, yeah, nobody ever suggested getting me actual help. It was just assumed I'd grow out of it, or that I'd figure it out eventually if I just tried harder. Which I guess I sort of faked my way through enough to get through high school and some of college, just by the skin of my teeth. But now... yeah. Now it's just me and Dave and a mountain of mismatched insurance claims, and no color-coded folder is going to save my job. I actually went to the store and bought a planner a few weeks ago, thinking maybe it would help me track my processing times for HR, but I haven't even opened it. It's just sitting on my desk right now. So, no, to answer your question, nobody ever thought it was a medical thing back then.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at elementary school, when you think about all these attention and restlessness problems—the daydreaming, losing papers, needing constant reminders—do you remember that actually getting in the way of your grades or your ability to keep up with what the other kids were doing academically, even if you eventually managed to pass?\n\nPatient: I mean... no, not really. Like I mentioned before, I was mostly getting Bs and Cs, so I wasn't failing anything. It really didn't get in the way of my actual grades or stop me from moving up to the next grade level with all my friends. I was never in remedial classes or anything like that. Because, the thing is, elementary school academics just aren't that... demanding, I guess? \n\nLike, I could be completely zoned out while Mrs. Gable was explaining long division on the overhead projector, just staring at the dust motes floating in the light, completely in my own world. But then she'd hand out the worksheet, and I could usually just look at the example problem at the top of the page and kind of reverse-engineer it in my head well enough to get, you know, seven out of ten right. And that was a passing grade. So I kept up just fine. \n\nSame with spelling tests or vocabulary—I would literally just memorize the list on the bus ride to school on Friday morning. I'd just stare at the words for twenty minutes, take the test, get a B, and then immediately forget all of them by lunchtime. So on paper, if you just looked at my report cards, I looked like a totally average, middle-of-the-pack student. The teacher's comments in the margins would always say stuff like, \"A pleasure to have in class, but easily distracted,\" or \"Needs to pay better attention during quiet reading time,\" but the actual letter grades were completely fine. It never actually stopped me from keeping up with what everyone else was doing. \n\nAnd I think that's why nobody caught on that I was struggling to pay attention, because the results were so... normal. I was smart enough to just sort of float by without really locking in. But that's the thing, right? You can't just float by when you're dealing with complex commercial liability policies. You can't just look at an example claim and reverse-engineer it in five minutes, because every single policy has different exclusions and riders, and if you miss one detail because you zoned out, you're costing the company thousands of dollars. And Dave knows that. So yeah, back then, no, it didn't hurt my grades at all. The daydreaming didn't cost me anything academically. But now it's costing me everything, and I just don't know how to fix it since I've literally never had to before.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, thinking about all these attention and restlessness problems together—the daydreaming, losing papers, needing reminders—did they actually cause you real problems that you or your parents noticed as being significant, like struggling to keep up with schoolwork despite trying, having trouble with friendships, or causing real tension at home? Or was it really just that your teachers found it frustrating but everything basically worked out okay for you?\n\nPatient: I know I probably sound like I’m just giving you the exact same answer again, but it really, truly was just that second thing you said. It was frustrating for the teachers, sure, but everything basically worked out totally fine for me. \n\nI didn't have any trouble with friendships at all, actually. Like, I was always right in the middle of whatever we were doing at recess. We'd play wall-ball or trade Pokémon cards by the chain-link fence, and, I mean, none of those kids cared if I forgot my spelling words or if my backpack was just a black hole of crumpled-up worksheets. Kids don't care about that stuff, you know? They just care if you're fun to play with. So socially, I was completely fine. I never felt left out or alienated or anything like that.\n\nAnd at home... I mean, I told you about the whole elaborate planner system my mom would try to set up every year, so she was definitely annoyed sometimes. But it wasn't like... real tension, if that makes sense? It wasn't this dark, heavy cloud hanging over our house. We'd still have family movie nights, we'd go get pizza on Fridays, and she wasn't constantly riding me about my focus. It was just an occasional, \"Oh, there he goes again, losing his homework\" kind of annoyance. \n\nSo it really just fell on the teachers to deal with it. And honestly, bless them, because I'm sure I was exhausting to have in class. I can literally still picture Mrs. Gable just closing her eyes and taking this slow, deep breath when she'd walk by and see me, like, drawing on the rubber part of my shoe instead of doing the silent reading assignment. But they just dealt with it. They'd tap my desk, point to the paragraph I was supposed to be on, and that was it. I'd get my C-plus and move on with my life. It never ruined anything for me back then. \n\nWhich is just... I don't know, it's honestly so frustrating to think about now. Because part of me wishes someone *had* made it a real problem back then. If it had been a huge deal when I was ten, maybe we would have figured something out, and maybe I wouldn't be sitting here at twenty-six, completely terrified that Dave is going to pull me into his office and fire me over these backed-up insurance claims. Because back then, the absolute highest stakes were just a heavy sigh from a teacher, and now the stakes are my rent and my entire livelihood, and I just... I don't have the tools for it. But yeah, to answer your question, it wasn't a real problem back then. I just sort of floated through.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned getting mostly Bs and Cs in elementary school, but I want to understand this better: compared to your classmates, were you actually keeping up academically at the same level they were, or were you falling behind and just managing to pass because you were smart enough to catch up at the last minute?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess it’s kind of hard to say exactly where I was compared to everyone else, because I wasn't really paying attention to what they were doing either. But... if I had to really think about it, I’d say I was keeping up just fine. It wasn't like I was drowning and then suddenly pulling off a miracle at the end of the semester. It was more like... I was just sort of bobbing along with the current, you know? \n\nLike, I remember in fourth grade, we had this big unit on the Oregon Trail. Everyone was obsessed with it because we got to play the computer game during free time. And the actual assignment was to keep this journal from the perspective of a pioneer. Some kids, like this girl Sarah who sat next to me, she had this incredibly detailed journal with drawings of the wagon and lists of provisions and she even burned the edges of the paper to make it look old. She was definitely operating on a different level. But then there were kids who just wrote, \"Today we crossed a river. An ox died.\" And I was somewhere right in the middle. I’d write enough to show I understood the assignment, maybe throw in a detail about dysentery because it was funny, and that was it. I got a B. I wasn't falling behind the kid who wrote about the dead ox, but I definitely wasn't doing what Sarah was doing. \n\nSo, I don't think I was falling behind. I was just doing exactly what was required to stay in the middle of the pack. I didn't feel like I was constantly playing catch-up, mostly because I just didn't care enough to try and be at the top. It was just... I’d do the bare minimum, get my C-plus or B-minus, and then go out to recess. And it worked. It worked perfectly fine for years. Until it didn't. Because now, with these claims, Dave isn't grading on a curve. He expects Sarah-level attention to detail on every single file, and I’m still just writing about the dead ox, you know? And I can't figure out how to bridge that gap. So, yeah, back then, I was definitely keeping up with the average kid, just not... excelling, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you look back at your elementary school years, do you think you were actually performing at the level you were capable of academically, or do you think you were underperforming compared to what you could have done if you had been able to focus better?\n\nPatient: I mean... honestly? If I really think about it, I definitely wasn't performing at the level I was capable of. I was totally underperforming, even if it didn't look like it on paper because I was getting those Bs and Cs. Because, like I said, I was bright enough to just sort of coast. \n\nBut looking back at it now, with everything going on at work with Dave and the insurance policies... it's like this giant lightbulb just went off, you know? Because this feeling of just barely skating by, of not living up to my potential... this wasn't brand new for me. It really wasn't. Like, I remember—and this is so clear now that I'm actually thinking about it—I remember my mom keeping all my report cards in this big accordion folder in the bottom drawer of the filing cabinet in the den. And every single year, from like second grade all the way through fifth, the teachers would write the exact same kind of comments in that little box at the bottom. \n\nIt was always some variation of, \"He is a very bright boy, but...\" And then the \"but\" was always this laundry list of things that are literally exactly what I'm doing now at work. They'd write that I was bright, but I constantly lost my worksheets. Or that I was smart, but I always missed the verbal instructions when she was standing right at the front of the room talking to us. Or they'd say I needed constant reminders just to finish basic classwork that everyone else was already done with. \n\nLike, I remember Mrs. Gable—I keep bringing her up, but she was my fourth-grade teacher and she was so strict—she actually wrote in red pen once, \"He has so much potential if he could just apply himself and keep track of his assignments.\" And my mom was so annoyed, she made me sit at the kitchen table and reorganize my entire Trapper Keeper, which took like three hours because I had shoved so many loose, half-finished math sheets into the pockets that they were all ripped and crinkled. \n\nSo, yeah. I was smart enough to pass, and I guess that's why no one sounded the alarm. But I definitely wasn't doing what I could have been doing if I had just been able to focus and keep track of my stuff like a normal kid. And it's just wild to me that I'm sitting here at twenty-six, and my boss is essentially telling me the exact same thing Mrs. Gable told my mom when I was ten. It's like I've been stuck in this same loop my entire life, just losing papers and missing instructions, and I never even realized it until my job was on the line."
